From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia
Spectral density of light emitted by a black body
Not to be confused with [Planck relation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%E2%80%93Einstein_relation "Planck–Einstein relation") or [Planck's principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_principle "Planck's principle").
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Black_body.svg/330px-Black_body.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Black_body.svg)Planck's law accurately describes black-body radiation. Shown here are a family of curves for different temperatures. The classical (black) curve diverges from observed intensity at high frequencies (short wavelengths).
In [physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics "Physics"), **Planck's law** (also **Planck radiation law**[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-young1_2016_1256-1257-1): 1305 ) describes the [spectral density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectrum_\(physical_sciences\) "Spectrum \(physical sciences\)") of [electromagnetic radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electromagnetic_radiation "Electromagnetic radiation") emitted by a [black body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_body "Black body") in [thermal equilibrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_equilibrium "Thermal equilibrium") at a given [temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperature "Temperature") T, when there is no net flow of [matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matter "Matter") or [energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy "Energy") between the body and its environment.[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Planck_1914_42-2)
At the end of the 19th century, physicists were unable to explain why the observed spectrum of [black-body radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-body_radiation "Black-body radiation"), which by then had been accurately measured, diverged significantly at higher [frequencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frequencies "Frequencies") from that predicted by existing theories. In 1900, German physicist [Max Planck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Planck "Max Planck") heuristically[[a]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-3) derived a formula for the observed spectrum by assuming that a hypothetical electrically charged [oscillator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscillation "Oscillation") in a cavity that contained black-body radiation could only change its [energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy "Energy") in a minimal increment, E, that was proportional to the frequency of its associated [electromagnetic wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electromagnetic_wave "Electromagnetic wave"). While Planck originally regarded the hypothesis of dividing energy into increments as a mathematical artifice, introduced merely to get the correct answer, other physicists including [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein") built on his work, and Planck's insight is now recognized to be of fundamental importance to [quantum theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_mechanics "Quantum mechanics"). 
## Definition
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: Definition")]
Every [physical body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_body "Physical body") spontaneously and continuously emits [electromagnetic radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electromagnetic_radiation "Electromagnetic radiation") and the [spectral radiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectral_radiance "Spectral radiance") of a body, _B ν_, describes the spectral emissive power per unit area, per unit solid angle and per unit frequency for particular radiation frequencies. The relationship given by Planck's radiation law, given below, shows that with increasing temperature, the total radiated energy of a body increases and the peak of the emitted spectrum shifts to shorter wavelengths.[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaofeng_Shao,_et_al.2019\[httpshalarchives-ouvertesfrhal-02308467documentpage7_6\]-4) According to Planck's distribution law, the spectral [energy density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_density "Energy density") (energy per unit volume per unit frequency) at given temperature is given by:[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-5)[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-:0-6)
u ν ( ν , T ) = 8 π h ν 3 c 3 1 e h ν k B T − 1 . {\displaystyle u_{\nu }(\nu ,T)={\frac {8\pi h\nu ^{3}}{c^{3}}}{\frac {1}{e^{\frac {h\nu }{k_{\mathrm {B} }T}}-1}}.} ![{\\displaystyle u_{\\nu }\(\\nu ,T\)={\\frac {8\\pi h\\nu ^{3}}{c^{3}}}{\\frac {1}{e^{\\frac {h\\nu }{k_{\\mathrm {B} }T}}-1}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2f0a36f9c2c197e14804d314587edcc8819ac2b0)
Alternatively, the law can be expressed for the [spectral radiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectral_radiance "Spectral radiance") of a body for [frequency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frequency "Frequency") _ν_ at [absolute temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_temperature "Absolute temperature") _T_ given as:[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Planck_1914_6_168-7)[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Chan8-8)[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Rybicki_1979_22-9)
B ν ( ν , T ) = 2 h ν 3 c 2 1 e h ν k B T − 1 {\displaystyle B_{\nu }(\nu ,T)={\frac {2h\nu ^{3}}{c^{2}}}{\frac {1}{e^{\frac {h\nu }{k_{\mathrm {B} }T}}-1}}} ![{\\displaystyle B_{\\nu }\(\\nu ,T\)={\\frac {2h\\nu ^{3}}{c^{2}}}{\\frac {1}{e^{\\frac {h\\nu }{k_{\\mathrm {B} }T}}-1}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/732a1339c992084dcad37b9c1cf90044d8065e62)
where _k_ B is the [Boltzmann constant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boltzmann_constant "Boltzmann constant"), _h_ is the [Planck constant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck_constant "Planck constant"), and _c_ is the [speed of light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speed_of_light "Speed of light") in the medium, whether material or vacuum. 
The SI units of the spectral [radiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiance "Radiance") _B ν_ are [W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watt "Watt")·[sr-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steradian "Steradian")·[m-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metre "Metre")·[Hz-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hertz "Hertz"). The spectral [radiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiance "Radiance") _B λ_ are [W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watt "Watt")·[sr-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steradian "Steradian")·[m-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metre "Metre"). 
The cgs units of spectral [radiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiance "Radiance") _B ν_ are [erg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erg "Erg")·[s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second "Second")−1·[sr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steradian "Steradian")−1·[cm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centimetre "Centimetre")−2·[Hz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hertz "Hertz")−1. 
The terms _B_ and _u_ are related to each other by a factor of ⁠4π/_c_ ⁠, since _B_ is independent of direction and radiation travels at speed _c_. 
The spectral radiance can also be expressed per unit [wavelength](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavelength "Wavelength") _λ_ instead of per unit frequency. In addition, the law may be expressed in other terms, such as the number of photons emitted at a certain wavelength, or the energy density in a volume of radiation. 
In the limit of low frequencies (i.e. long wavelengths), Planck's law tends to the [Rayleigh–Jeans law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rayleigh%E2%80%93Jeans_law "Rayleigh–Jeans law"), while in the limit of high frequencies (i.e. small wavelengths) it tends to the [Wien approximation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wien_approximation "Wien approximation"). 
Max Planck developed the law in 1900 with only empirically determined constants, and later showed that, expressed as an energy distribution, it is the unique [stable distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stable_distribution "Stable distribution") for radiation in [thermodynamic equilibrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermodynamic_equilibrium "Thermodynamic equilibrium").[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Planck_1914_42-2) As an energy distribution, it is one of a family of thermal equilibrium distributions which include the [Bose–Einstein distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bose%E2%80%93Einstein_distribution "Bose–Einstein distribution"), the [Fermi–Dirac distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fermi%E2%80%93Dirac_distribution "Fermi–Dirac distribution") and the [Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxwell%E2%80%93Boltzmann_distribution "Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution"). 
## Black-body radiation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: Black-body radiation")]
Main article: [Black-body radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-body_radiation "Black-body radiation")
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/EffectiveTemperature_300dpi_e.png/330px-EffectiveTemperature_300dpi_e.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:EffectiveTemperature_300dpi_e.png)The [Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun "Sun") approximates a black body radiator. Its [effective temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effective_temperature "Effective temperature") is about 
A black-body is an idealised object which absorbs and emits all radiation frequencies. Near [thermodynamic equilibrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermodynamic_equilibrium "Thermodynamic equilibrium"), the emitted radiation is closely described by Planck's law and because of its dependence on [temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperature "Temperature"), Planck radiation is said to be thermal radiation, such that the higher the temperature of a body the more radiation it emits at every wavelength. 
Planck radiation has a maximum intensity at a wavelength that depends on the temperature of the body. For example, at room temperature (~[K](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelvin "Kelvin")), a body emits thermal radiation that is mostly [infrared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared "Infrared") and invisible. At higher temperatures the amount of infrared radiation increases and can be felt as heat, and more visible radiation is emitted so the body glows visibly red. At higher temperatures, the body is bright yellow or blue-white and emits significant amounts of short wavelength radiation, including [ultraviolet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraviolet "Ultraviolet") and even [x-rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-ray "X-ray"). The surface of the Sun (~[Wien's displacement law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wien%27s_displacement_law "Wien's displacement law"). 
Planck radiation is the greatest amount of radiation that any body at thermal equilibrium can emit from its surface, whatever its chemical composition or surface structure.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Stewart_1858-10) The passage of radiation across an interface between media can be characterized by the [emissivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emissivity "Emissivity") of the interface (the ratio of the actual [radiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiance "Radiance") to the theoretical Planck radiance), usually denoted by the symbol _ε_. It is in general dependent on chemical composition and physical structure, on temperature, on the wavelength, on the angle of passage, and on the [polarization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polarization_\(waves\) "Polarization \(waves\)").[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-11) The emissivity of a natural interface is always between _ε_ = 0 and 1. 
A body that interfaces with another medium which both has _ε_ = 1 and absorbs all the radiation incident upon it is said to be a black body. The surface of a black body can be modelled by a small hole in the wall of a large enclosure which is maintained at a uniform temperature with opaque walls that, at every wavelength, are not perfectly reflective. At equilibrium, the radiation inside this enclosure is described by Planck's law, as is the radiation leaving the small hole. 
Just as the [Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxwell%E2%80%93Boltzmann_distribution "Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution") is the unique maximum [entropy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entropy "Entropy") energy distribution for a gas of material particles at thermal equilibrium, so is Planck's distribution for a [gas of photons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photon_gas "Photon gas").[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Planck_1914-12)[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-13) By contrast to a material gas where the masses and number of particles play a role, the spectral radiance, pressure and energy density of a photon gas at thermal equilibrium are entirely determined by the temperature. 
If the photon gas is not Planckian, the [second law of thermodynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_law_of_thermodynamics "Second law of thermodynamics") guarantees that interactions (between photons and other particles or even, at sufficiently high temperatures, between the photons themselves) will cause the photon energy distribution to change and approach the Planck distribution. In such an approach to thermodynamic equilibrium, photons are created or annihilated in the right numbers and with the right energies to fill the cavity with a Planck distribution until they reach the equilibrium temperature. It is as if the gas is a mixture of sub-gases, one for every band of wavelengths, and each sub-gas eventually attains the common temperature. 
The quantity _B_ _ν_(_ν_ , _T_) is the [spectral radiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectral_radiance "Spectral radiance") as a function of temperature and frequency. It has units of [W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watt "Watt")·[m](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metre "Metre")−2·[sr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steradian "Steradian")−1·[Hz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hertz "Hertz")−1 in the [SI system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SI_units "SI units"). An infinitesimal amount of power _B_ _ν_(_ν_ , _T_) cos _θ_ _dA_ _d_ Ω _dν_ is radiated in the direction described by the angle θ from the surface normal from infinitesimal surface area _dA_ into infinitesimal solid angle _d_ Ω in an infinitesimal frequency band of width _dν_ centered on frequency ν. The total power radiated into any solid angle is the [integral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integral "Integral") of _B ν_(_ν_ , _T_) over those three quantities, and is given by the [Stefan–Boltzmann law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stefan%E2%80%93Boltzmann_law "Stefan–Boltzmann law"). The spectral radiance of Planckian radiation from a black body has the same value for every direction and angle of polarization, and so the black body is said to be a [Lambertian radiator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambert%27s_cosine_law "Lambert's cosine law"). 
## Different forms
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Different forms")]
Planck's law can be encountered in several forms depending on the conventions and preferences of different scientific fields. The various forms of the law for spectral radiance are summarized in the table below. Forms on the left are most often encountered in [experimental fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimental_physics "Experimental physics"), while those on the right are most often encountered in [theoretical fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theoretical_physics "Theoretical physics").

These distributions represent the spectral radiance of blackbodies—the power emitted from the emitting surface, per unit projected area of emitting surface, per unit [solid angle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_angle "Solid angle"), per spectral unit (frequency, wavelength, wavenumber or their angular equivalents, or fractional frequency or wavelength). Since the radiance is [isotropic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotropy "Isotropy") (i.e. independent of direction), the power emitted at an angle to the [normal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_\(geometry\) "Normal \(geometry\)") is proportional to the projected area, and therefore to the cosine of that angle as per [Lambert's cosine law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambert%27s_cosine_law "Lambert's cosine law"), and is [unpolarized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polarization_\(waves\) "Polarization \(waves\)"). 
### Correspondence between spectral variable forms
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Correspondence between spectral variable forms")]
Different spectral variables require different corresponding forms of expression of the law. In general, one may not convert between the various forms of Planck's law simply by substituting one variable for another, because this would not take into account that the different forms have different units. Wavelength and frequency units are reciprocal. 
Corresponding forms of expression are related because they express one and the same physical fact: for a particular physical spectral increment, a corresponding particular physical energy increment is radiated. 
This is so whether it is expressed in terms of an increment of frequency, d _ν_ , or, correspondingly, of wavelength, d _λ_ , or of fractional bandwidth, d _ν_ /_ν_ or d _λ_ /_λ_. Introduction of a minus sign can indicate that an increment of frequency corresponds with decrement of wavelength. 
In order to convert the corresponding forms so that they express the same quantity in the same units we multiply by the spectral increment. Then, for a particular spectral increment, the particular physical energy increment may be written  B λ ( λ , T ) d λ = − B ν ( ν ( λ ) , T ) d ν , {\displaystyle B_{\lambda }(\lambda ,T)\,d\lambda =-B_{\nu }(\nu (\lambda ),T)\,d\nu ,} ![{\\displaystyle B_{\\lambda }\(\\lambda ,T\)\\,d\\lambda =-B_{\\nu }\(\\nu \(\\lambda \),T\)\\,d\\nu ,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a166306d26ba8aa0691edcacdf9e146cfeae5e2a) which leads to  B λ ( λ , T ) = − d ν d λ B ν ( ν ( λ ) , T ) . {\displaystyle B_{\lambda }(\lambda ,T)=-{\frac {d\nu }{d\lambda }}B_{\nu }(\nu (\lambda ),T).} ![{\\displaystyle B_{\\lambda }\(\\lambda ,T\)=-{\\frac {d\\nu }{d\\lambda }}B_{\\nu }\(\\nu \(\\lambda \),T\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/258c0e154bfc41645e66e8d7e255d21dc7bdf1ba)
Also, _ν_(_λ_) = ⁠ _c_ /_λ_ ⁠, so that ⁠ _dν_ /_dλ_ ⁠ = − ⁠ _c_ /_λ_ 2⁠. Substitution gives the correspondence between the frequency and wavelength forms, with their different dimensions and units.[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-SharkovConversions-16)[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-19) Consequently,  B λ ( T ) B ν ( T ) = c λ 2 = ν 2 c . {\displaystyle {\frac {B_{\lambda }(T)}{B_{\nu }(T)}}={\frac {c}{\lambda ^{2}}}={\frac {\nu ^{2}}{c}}.} ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {B_{\\lambda }\(T\)}{B_{\\nu }\(T\)}}={\\frac {c}{\\lambda ^{2}}}={\\frac {\\nu ^{2}}{c}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/baa85fe6fe919b122484378c4c6c93abcf16d3cb)
Evidently, the location of the peak of the spectral distribution for Planck's law depends on the choice of spectral variable. Nevertheless, in a manner of speaking, this formula means that the shape of the spectral distribution is independent of temperature, according to Wien's displacement law, as detailed below in _[§ Properties §§ Percentiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#Percentiles)_. 
The fractional bandwidth form is related to the other forms by[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Marr2012-17)      B ln ⁡ x = ν B ν = λ B λ {\displaystyle B_{\ln x}=\nu B_{\nu }=\lambda B_{\lambda }} ![{\\displaystyle B_{\\ln x}=\\nu B_{\\nu }=\\lambda B_{\\lambda }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/eca97162742a2797524a5d629b2e25152c81d4eb).
### First and second radiation constants
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: First and second radiation constants")]
In the above variants of Planck's law, the _wavelength_ and _wavenumber_ variants use the terms 2 _hc_ 2 and ⁠ _hc_ /_k_ B⁠ which comprise physical constants only. Consequently, these terms can be considered as physical constants themselves,[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-20) and are therefore referred to as the **first radiation constant** _c_ 1 _L_ and the **second radiation constant** _c_ 2 with 
_c_ 1 _L_ = 2 _hc_ 2
and 
_c_ 2 = ⁠ _hc_ /_k_ B⁠.
Using the radiation constants, the _wavelength_ variant of Planck's law can be simplified to  L ( λ , T ) = c 1 L λ 5 1 exp ⁡ ( c 2 λ T ) − 1 {\displaystyle L(\lambda ,T)={\frac {c_{1L}}{\lambda ^{5}}}{\frac {1}{\exp \left({\frac {c_{2}}{\lambda T}}\right)-1}}} ![{\\displaystyle L\(\\lambda ,T\)={\\frac {c_{1L}}{\\lambda ^{5}}}{\\frac {1}{\\exp \\left\({\\frac {c_{2}}{\\lambda T}}\\right\)-1}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d4837b611652f7189284e62291123f661a4ea9d6) and the _wavenumber_ variant can be simplified correspondingly. 
_L_ is used here instead of _B_ because it is the SI symbol for _spectral radiance_.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] The _L_ in _c_ 1 _L_ refers to that. This reference is necessary because Planck's law can be reformulated to give [spectral radiant exitance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectral_radiant_exitance "Spectral radiant exitance") _M_(_λ_ , _T_) rather than _spectral radiance_ _L_(_λ_ , _T_), in which case _c_ 1 replaces _c_ 1 _L_ , with 
_c_ 1 = 2π _hc_ 2,
so that Planck's law for _spectral radiant exitance_ can be written as  M ( λ , T ) = c 1 λ 5 1 exp ⁡ ( c 2 λ T ) − 1 {\displaystyle M(\lambda ,T)={\frac {c_{1}}{\lambda ^{5}}}{\frac {1}{\exp \left({\frac {c_{2}}{\lambda T}}\right)-1}}} ![{\\displaystyle M\(\\lambda ,T\)={\\frac {c_{1}}{\\lambda ^{5}}}{\\frac {1}{\\exp \\left\({\\frac {c_{2}}{\\lambda T}}\\right\)-1}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d92629058bfd9caed108073882e20cd6ce6349ca)
As measuring techniques have improved, the [General Conference on Weights and Measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Conference_on_Weights_and_Measures "General Conference on Weights and Measures") has revised its estimate of _c_ 2; see _[Planckian locus § International Temperature Scale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planckian_locus#International_Temperature_Scale "Planckian locus")_ for details. 
## Physics
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Physics")]
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/BlackbodyEnergies.svg/330px-BlackbodyEnergies.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:BlackbodyEnergies.svg)Freeze-out of high-energy oscillators
Planck's law describes the unique and characteristic spectral distribution for electromagnetic radiation in thermodynamic equilibrium, when there is no net flow of matter or energy.[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Planck_1914_42-2) Its physics is most easily understood by considering the radiation in a cavity with rigid opaque walls. Motion of the walls can affect the radiation. If the walls are not opaque, then the thermodynamic equilibrium is not isolated. It is of interest to explain how the thermodynamic equilibrium is attained. There are two main cases: (a) when the approach to thermodynamic equilibrium is in the presence of matter, when the walls of the cavity are imperfectly reflective for every wavelength or when the walls are perfectly reflective while the cavity contains a small black body (this was the main case considered by Planck); or (b) when the approach to equilibrium is in the absence of matter, when the walls are perfectly reflective for all wavelengths and the cavity contains no matter. For matter not enclosed in such a cavity, thermal radiation can be approximately explained by appropriate use of Planck's law. 
Classical physics led, via the [equipartition theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equipartition_theorem "Equipartition theorem"), to the [ultraviolet catastrophe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraviolet_catastrophe "Ultraviolet catastrophe"), a prediction that the total blackbody radiation intensity was infinite. If supplemented by the classically unjustifiable assumption that for some reason the radiation is finite, classical thermodynamics provides an account of some aspects of the Planck distribution, such as the [Stefan–Boltzmann law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stefan%E2%80%93Boltzmann_law "Stefan–Boltzmann law"), and the [Wien displacement law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wien_displacement_law "Wien displacement law"). For the case of the presence of matter, quantum mechanics provides a good account, as found below in the section headed [Einstein coefficients](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#Physics#Einstein_coefficients). This was the case considered by Einstein, and is nowadays used for [quantum optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_optics "Quantum optics").[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-21)[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-22) For the case of the absence of matter, quantum field theory is necessary, because non-relativistic quantum mechanics with fixed particle numbers does not provide a sufficient account. 
### Photons
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Photons")]
Quantum theoretical explanation of Planck's law views the radiation as a gas of massless, uncharged, bosonic particles, namely photons, in [thermodynamic equilibrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermodynamic_equilibrium "Thermodynamic equilibrium"). Photons are viewed as the carriers of the electromagnetic interaction between electrically charged elementary particles. Photon numbers are not conserved. Photons are created or annihilated in the right numbers and with the right energies to fill the cavity with photons described by the Planck distribution. For a photon gas in thermodynamic equilibrium, the [internal energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_energy "Internal energy") density is entirely determined by the temperature; moreover, the pressure is entirely determined by the internal energy density. This is unlike the case of thermodynamic equilibrium for material gases, for which the internal energy is determined not only by the temperature, but also, independently, by the respective numbers of the different molecules, and independently again, by the specific characteristics of the different molecules. For different material gases at given temperature, the pressure and internal energy density can vary independently, because different molecules can carry independently different excitation energies. 
Planck's law arises as a limit of the [Bose–Einstein distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bose%E2%80%93Einstein_distribution "Bose–Einstein distribution"), the energy distribution describing non-interactive [bosons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosons "Bosons") in thermodynamic equilibrium. In the case of massless bosons such as [photons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photons "Photons") and [gluons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gluons "Gluons"), the [chemical potential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_potential "Chemical potential") is zero and the Bose–Einstein distribution reduces to the Planck distribution. There is another fundamental equilibrium energy distribution: the [Fermi–Dirac distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fermi%E2%80%93Dirac_distribution "Fermi–Dirac distribution"), which describes [fermions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fermions "Fermions"), such as electrons, in thermal equilibrium. The two distributions differ because multiple bosons can occupy the same [quantum state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_state "Quantum state"), while multiple fermions cannot. At low densities, the number of available quantum states per particle is large, and this difference becomes irrelevant. In the low density limit, the Bose–Einstein and the Fermi–Dirac distribution each reduce to the [Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxwell%E2%80%93Boltzmann_distribution "Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution"). 
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: Kirchhoff's law of thermal radiation")]
Main article: [Kirchhoff's law of thermal radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirchhoff%27s_law_of_thermal_radiation "Kirchhoff's law of thermal radiation")
Kirchhoff's law of thermal radiation is a succinct and brief account of a complicated physical situation. The following is an introductory sketch of that situation, and is very far from being a rigorous physical argument. The purpose here is only to summarize the main physical factors in the situation, and the main conclusions. 
#### Spectral dependence of thermal radiation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: Spectral dependence of thermal radiation")]
There is a difference between [conductive heat transfer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conductive_heat_transfer "Conductive heat transfer") and [radiative heat transfer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiative_heat_transfer "Radiative heat transfer"). Radiative heat transfer can be filtered to pass only a definite band of radiative frequencies. 
It is generally known that the hotter a body becomes, the more thermal radiation it emits at every frequency. 
In a cavity in an opaque body with rigid walls that are not perfectly reflective at any frequency, in thermodynamic equilibrium, there is only one temperature, and it must be shared in common by the radiation of every frequency. 
One may imagine two such cavities, each in its own isolated radiative and thermodynamic equilibrium. One may imagine an optical device that allows radiative heat transfer between the two cavities, filtered to pass only a definite band of radiative frequencies. If the values of the spectral radiances of the radiations in the cavities differ in that frequency band, heat may be expected to pass from the hotter to the colder. One might propose to use such a filtered transfer of heat in such a band to drive a [heat engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_engine "Heat engine"). If the two bodies are at the same temperature, the second law of thermodynamics does not allow the heat engine to work. It may be inferred that for a temperature common to the two bodies, the values of the spectral radiances in the pass-band must also be common. This must hold for every frequency band.[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-23)[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-24)[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-25) This became clear to Balfour Stewart and later to Kirchhoff. Balfour Stewart found experimentally that of all surfaces, one of [lamp-black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_black "Carbon black") emitted the greatest amount of thermal radiation for every quality of radiation, judged by various filters. 
Thinking theoretically, Kirchhoff went a little further and pointed out that this implied that the spectral radiance, as a function of radiative frequency, of any such cavity in thermodynamic equilibrium must be a unique universal function of temperature. He postulated an ideal black body that interfaced with its surroundings in just such a way as to absorb all the radiation that falls on it. By the Helmholtz reciprocity principle, radiation from the interior of such a body would pass unimpeded directly to its surroundings without reflection at the interface. In thermodynamic equilibrium, the thermal radiation emitted from such a body would have that unique universal spectral radiance as a function of temperature. This insight is the root of Kirchhoff's law of thermal radiation. 
#### Relation between absorptivity and emissivity
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: Relation between absorptivity and emissivity")]
One may imagine a small homogeneous spherical material body labeled _X_ at a temperature _T X_, lying in a radiation field within a large cavity with walls of material labeled _Y_ at a temperature _T Y_. The body _X_ emits its own thermal radiation. At a particular frequency _ν_ , the radiation emitted from a particular cross-section through the centre of _X_ in one sense in a direction normal to that cross-section may be denoted _I_ _ν_ ,_X_(_T X_), characteristically for the material of _X_. At that frequency _ν_ , the radiative power from the walls into that cross-section in the opposite sense in that direction may be denoted _I_ _ν_ ,_Y_(_T Y_), for the wall temperature _T Y_. For the material of _X_ , defining the absorptivity _α_ _ν_ ,_X_ ,_Y_(_T X, TY_) as the fraction of that incident radiation absorbed by _X_ , that incident energy is absorbed at a rate _α_ _ν_ ,_X_ ,_Y_(_T X_, _T Y_) _I_ _ν_ ,_Y_(_T Y_). 
The rate _q_(_ν_ ,_T X_,_T Y_) of accumulation of energy in one sense into the cross-section of the body can then be expressed  q ( ν , T X , T Y ) = α ν , X , Y ( T X , T Y ) I ν , Y ( T Y ) − I ν , X ( T X ) . {\displaystyle q(\nu ,T_{X},T_{Y})=\alpha _{\nu ,X,Y}(T_{X},T_{Y})I_{\nu ,Y}(T_{Y})-I_{\nu ,X}(T_{X}).} ![{\\displaystyle q\(\\nu ,T_{X},T_{Y}\)=\\alpha _{\\nu ,X,Y}\(T_{X},T_{Y}\)I_{\\nu ,Y}\(T_{Y}\)-I_{\\nu ,X}\(T_{X}\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a2115c538454bf0b1253c90223261c2a447efdbc)
Kirchhoff's seminal insight, mentioned just above, was that, at thermodynamic equilibrium at temperature _T_ , there exists a unique universal radiative distribution, nowadays denoted _B ν_(_T_), that is independent of the chemical characteristics of the materials _X_ and _Y_ , that leads to a very valuable understanding of the radiative exchange equilibrium of any body at all, as follows. 
When there is thermodynamic equilibrium at temperature _T_ , the cavity radiation from the walls has that unique universal value, so that _I_ _ν_ ,_Y_(_T Y_) = _B ν_(_T_). Further, one may define the emissivity _ε_ _ν_ ,_X_(_T X_) of the material of the body _X_ just so that at thermodynamic equilibrium at temperature _T X_ = _T_ , one has _I_ _ν_ ,_X_(_T X_) = _I_ _ν_ ,_X_(_T_) = _ε_ _ν_ ,_X_(_T_) _B ν_(_T_). 
When thermal equilibrium prevails at temperature _T_ = _T X_ = _T Y_, the rate of accumulation of energy vanishes so that _q_(_ν_ ,_T X_,_T Y_) = 0. It follows that in thermodynamic equilibrium, when _T_ = _T X_ = _T Y_,  0 = α ν , X , Y ( T , T ) B ν ( T ) − ϵ ν , X ( T ) B ν ( T ) . {\displaystyle 0=\alpha _{\nu ,X,Y}(T,T)B_{\nu }(T)-\epsilon _{\nu ,X}(T)B_{\nu }(T).} ![{\\displaystyle 0=\\alpha _{\\nu ,X,Y}\(T,T\)B_{\\nu }\(T\)-\\epsilon _{\\nu ,X}\(T\)B_{\\nu }\(T\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/68bef857ef6d90251db5ec180c42f87147d461fa)
Kirchhoff pointed out that it follows that in thermodynamic equilibrium, when _T_ = _T X_ = _T Y_,  α ν , X , Y ( T , T ) = ϵ ν , X ( T ) . {\displaystyle \alpha _{\nu ,X,Y}(T,T)=\epsilon _{\nu ,X}(T).} ![{\\displaystyle \\alpha _{\\nu ,X,Y}\(T,T\)=\\epsilon _{\\nu ,X}\(T\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/54a80910730e996031a480f368fb1fd209984bab)
Introducing the special notation _α_ _ν_ ,_X_(_T_) for the absorptivity of material _X_ at thermodynamic equilibrium at temperature _T_ (justified by a discovery of Einstein, as indicated below), one further has the equality  α ν , X ( T ) = ϵ ν , X ( T ) {\displaystyle \alpha _{\nu ,X}(T)=\epsilon _{\nu ,X}(T)} ![{\\displaystyle \\alpha _{\\nu ,X}\(T\)=\\epsilon _{\\nu ,X}\(T\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6643535b2cbfd80ec8430551784c91f902af5b18) at thermodynamic equilibrium. 
The equality of absorptivity and emissivity here demonstrated is specific for thermodynamic equilibrium at temperature _T_ and is in general not to be expected to hold when conditions of thermodynamic equilibrium do not hold. The emissivity and absorptivity are each separately properties of the molecules of the material but they depend differently upon the distributions of states of molecular excitation on the occasion, because of a phenomenon known as "stimulated emission", that was discovered by Einstein. On occasions when the material is in thermodynamic equilibrium or in a state known as local thermodynamic equilibrium, the emissivity and absorptivity become equal. Very strong incident radiation or other factors can disrupt thermodynamic equilibrium or local thermodynamic equilibrium. Local thermodynamic equilibrium in a gas means that molecular collisions far outweigh light emission and absorption in determining the distributions of states of molecular excitation. 
Kirchhoff pointed out that he did not know the precise character of _B ν_(_T_), but he thought it important that it should be found out. Four decades after Kirchhoff's insight of the general principles of its existence and character, Planck's contribution was to determine the precise mathematical expression of that equilibrium distribution _B ν_(_T_). 
### Black body
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=11 "Edit section: Black body")]
Main article: [Black body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_body "Black body")
In physics, one considers an ideal black body, here labeled _B_ , defined as one that completely absorbs all of the electromagnetic radiation falling upon it at every frequency _ν_ (hence the term "black"). According to Kirchhoff's law of thermal radiation, this entails that, for every frequency _ν_ , at thermodynamic equilibrium at temperature _T_ , one has _α_ _ν_ ,_B_(_T_) = _ε_ _ν_ ,_B_(_T_) = 1, so that the thermal radiation from a black body is always equal to the full amount specified by Planck's law. No physical body can emit thermal radiation that exceeds that of a black body, since if it were in equilibrium with a radiation field, it would be emitting more energy than was incident upon it. 
Though perfectly black materials do not exist, in practice a black surface can be accurately approximated.[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Planck_1914_42-2) As to its material interior, a body of condensed matter, liquid, solid, or plasma, with a definite interface with its surroundings, is completely black to radiation if it is completely opaque. That means that it absorbs all of the radiation that penetrates the interface of the body with its surroundings, and enters the body. This is not too difficult to achieve in practice. On the other hand, a perfectly black interface is not found in nature. A perfectly black interface reflects no radiation, but transmits all that falls on it, from either side. The best practical way to make an effectively black interface is to simulate an 'interface' by a small hole in the wall of a large cavity in a completely opaque rigid body of material that does not reflect perfectly at any frequency, with its walls at a controlled temperature. Beyond these requirements, the component material of the walls is unrestricted. Radiation entering the hole has almost no possibility of escaping the cavity without being absorbed by multiple impacts with its walls.[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-26)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=12 "Edit section: Lambert's cosine law")]
Main article: [Lambert's cosine law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambert%27s_cosine_law "Lambert's cosine law")
As explained by Planck,[[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-27) a radiating body has an interior consisting of matter, and an interface with its contiguous neighbouring material medium, which is usually the medium from within which the radiation from the surface of the body is observed. The interface is not composed of physical matter but is a theoretical conception, a mathematical two-dimensional surface, a joint property of the two contiguous media, strictly speaking belonging to neither separately. Such an interface can neither absorb nor emit, because it is not composed of physical matter; but it is the site of reflection and transmission of radiation, because it is a surface of discontinuity of optical properties. The reflection and transmission of radiation at the interface obey the [Stokes–Helmholtz reciprocity principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmholtz_reciprocity "Helmholtz reciprocity"). 
At any point in the interior of a black body located inside a cavity in thermodynamic equilibrium at temperature _T_ the radiation is homogeneous, isotropic and unpolarized. A black body absorbs all and reflects none of the electromagnetic radiation incident upon it. According to the Helmholtz reciprocity principle, radiation from the interior of a black body is not reflected at its surface, but is fully transmitted to its exterior. Because of the isotropy of the radiation in the body's interior, the [spectral radiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectral_radiance "Spectral radiance") of radiation transmitted from its interior to its exterior through its surface is independent of direction.[[27]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-28)
This is expressed by saying that radiation from the surface of a black body in thermodynamic equilibrium obeys Lambert's cosine law.[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-29)[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-30) This means that the spectral flux _d_ Φ(_dA_ , _θ_ , _d_ Ω, _dν_) from a given infinitesimal element of area _dA_ of the actual emitting surface of the black body, detected from a given direction that makes an angle _θ_ with the normal to the actual emitting surface at _dA_ , into an element of solid angle of detection _d_ Ω centred on the direction indicated by _θ_ , in an element of frequency bandwidth _dν_ , can be represented as[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-31) d Φ ( d A , θ , d Ω , d ν ) d Ω = L 0 ( d A , d ν ) d A d ν cos ⁡ θ {\displaystyle {\frac {d\Phi (dA,\theta ,d\Omega ,d\nu )}{d\Omega }}=L^{0}(dA,d\nu )\,dA\,d\nu \,\cos \theta } ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {d\\Phi \(dA,\\theta ,d\\Omega ,d\\nu \)}{d\\Omega }}=L^{0}\(dA,d\\nu \)\\,dA\\,d\\nu \\,\\cos \\theta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b869515bdc9908bfcd7952ede8104e86017e3fe5) where _L_ 0(_dA_ , _dν_) denotes the flux, per unit area per unit frequency per unit solid angle, that area _dA_ would show if it were measured in its normal direction _θ_ = 0. 
The factor cos _θ_ is present because the area to which the spectral radiance refers directly is the projection, of the actual emitting surface area, onto a plane perpendicular to the direction indicated by _θ_ . This is the reason for the name _cosine law_. 
Taking into account the independence of direction of the spectral radiance of radiation from the surface of a black body in thermodynamic equilibrium, one has _L_ 0(_dA_ , _dν_) = _B_ _ν_(_T_) and so  d Φ ( d A , θ , d Ω , d ν ) d Ω = B ν ( T ) d A d ν cos ⁡ θ . {\displaystyle {\frac {d\Phi (dA,\theta ,d\Omega ,d\nu )}{d\Omega }}=B_{\nu }(T)\,dA\,d\nu \,\cos \theta .} ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {d\\Phi \(dA,\\theta ,d\\Omega ,d\\nu \)}{d\\Omega }}=B_{\\nu }\(T\)\\,dA\\,d\\nu \\,\\cos \\theta .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0a2de79e95caaf1e570364075e0cac72a7aa20a8)
Thus Lambert's cosine law expresses the independence of direction of the spectral radiance _B_ _ν_ (_T_) of the surface of a black body in thermodynamic equilibrium. 
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Stefan–Boltzmann law")]
Main article: [Stefan–Boltzmann law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stefan%E2%80%93Boltzmann_law "Stefan–Boltzmann law")
The total power emitted per unit area at the surface of a black body (_P_) may be found by integrating the black body spectral flux found from Lambert's law over all frequencies, and over the solid angles corresponding to a hemisphere (_h_) above the surface.  P = ∫ 0 ∞ d ν ∫ h d Ω B ν cos ⁡ ( θ ) {\displaystyle P=\int _{0}^{\infty }d\nu \int _{h}d\Omega \,B_{\nu }\cos(\theta )} ![{\\displaystyle P=\\int _{0}^{\\infty }d\\nu \\int _{h}d\\Omega \\,B_{\\nu }\\cos\(\\theta \)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/55800d37f2ff9df426fe166982ca58f8736d2408)
The infinitesimal solid angle can be expressed in [spherical polar coordinates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spherical_polar_coordinates "Spherical polar coordinates"):  d Ω = sin ⁡ ( θ ) d θ d ϕ . {\displaystyle d\Omega =\sin(\theta )\,d\theta \,d\phi .} ![{\\displaystyle d\\Omega =\\sin\(\\theta \)\\,d\\theta \\,d\\phi .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d0ab72731d3785ae43fe044a45cfb3a2b17ccbae) So that:  P = ∫ 0 ∞ d ν ∫ 0 π 2 d θ ∫ 0 2 π d ϕ B ν ( T ) cos ⁡ ( θ ) sin ⁡ ( θ ) = σ T 4 {\displaystyle P=\int _{0}^{\infty }d\nu \int _{0}^{\tfrac {\pi }{2}}d\theta \int _{0}^{2\pi }d\phi \,B_{\nu }(T)\cos(\theta )\sin(\theta )=\sigma T^{4}} ![{\\displaystyle P=\\int _{0}^{\\infty }d\\nu \\int _{0}^{\\tfrac {\\pi }{2}}d\\theta \\int _{0}^{2\\pi }d\\phi \\,B_{\\nu }\(T\)\\cos\(\\theta \)\\sin\(\\theta \)=\\sigma T^{4}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ab81e1994d0604b2f5615f01f59d3846538a5f5c) where  σ = 2 k B 4 π 5 15 c 2 h 3 ≈ 5.670374419 × 10 − 8 J s − 1 m − 2 K − 4 {\displaystyle \sigma ={\frac {2k_{\mathrm {B} }^{4}\pi ^{5}}{15c^{2}h^{3}}}\approx 5.670374419\times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm {J\,s^{-1}m^{-2}K^{-4}} } ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma ={\\frac {2k_{\\mathrm {B} }^{4}\\pi ^{5}}{15c^{2}h^{3}}}\\approx 5.670374419\\times 10^{-8}\\,\\mathrm {J\\,s^{-1}m^{-2}K^{-4}} }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3fd419f3561c04d4bef6b45e13b1a8278595fc12) is known as the [Stefan–Boltzmann constant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stefan%E2%80%93Boltzmann_constant "Stefan–Boltzmann constant").[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Rybicki_1979_19-32)
### Radiative transfer
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section: Radiative transfer")]
Main article: [Radiative transfer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiative_transfer "Radiative transfer")
The equation of radiative transfer describes the way in which radiation is affected as it travels through a material medium. For the special case in which the material medium is in [thermodynamic equilibrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermodynamic_equilibrium "Thermodynamic equilibrium") in the neighborhood of a point in the medium, Planck's law is of special importance. 
For simplicity, we can consider the linear steady state, without [scattering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scattering "Scattering"). The equation of radiative transfer states that for a beam of light going through a small distance d _s_ , energy is conserved: The change in the (spectral) [radiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiance "Radiance") of that beam (_I ν_) is equal to the amount removed by the material medium plus the amount gained from the material medium. If the radiation field is in equilibrium with the material medium, these two contributions will be equal. The material medium will have a certain [emission coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emission_coefficient "Emission coefficient") and [absorption coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absorption_coefficient "Absorption coefficient"). 
The absorption coefficient _α_ is the fractional change in the intensity of the light beam as it travels the distance d _s_ , and has units of length−1. It is composed of two parts, the decrease due to absorption and the increase due to [stimulated emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stimulated_emission "Stimulated emission"). Stimulated emission is emission by the material body which is caused by and is proportional to the incoming radiation. It is included in the absorption term because, like absorption, it is proportional to the intensity of the incoming radiation. Since the amount of absorption will generally vary linearly as the density _ρ_ of the material, we may define a "mass absorption coefficient" _κ ν_ = ⁠ _α_ /_ρ_ ⁠ which is a property of the material itself. The change in intensity of a light beam due to absorption as it traverses a small distance d _s_ will then be[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Chan8-8) d I ν = − κ ν ρ I ν d s {\displaystyle dI_{\nu }=-\kappa _{\nu }\rho I_{\nu }\,ds} ![{\\displaystyle dI_{\\nu }=-\\kappa _{\\nu }\\rho I_{\\nu }\\,ds}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8e77e6cb7139291f2a1f21787f451480b1c7f529)
The "mass emission coefficient" _j ν_ is equal to the radiance per unit volume of a small [volume element](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volume_element "Volume element") divided by its mass (since, as for the mass absorption coefficient, the emission is proportional to the emitting mass) and has units of power⋅solid angle−1⋅frequency−1⋅density−1. Like the mass absorption coefficient, it too is a property of the material itself. The change in a light beam as it traverses a small distance d _s_ will then be[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Chan7-33) d I ν = j ν ρ d s {\displaystyle dI_{\nu }=j_{\nu }\rho \,ds} ![{\\displaystyle dI_{\\nu }=j_{\\nu }\\rho \\,ds}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0cb191d2f6b124375e0ff510119c8be271033266)
The equation of radiative transfer will then be the sum of these two contributions:[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Chan9-34) d I ν d s = j ν ρ − κ ν ρ I ν . {\displaystyle {\frac {dI_{\nu }}{ds}}=j_{\nu }\rho -\kappa _{\nu }\rho I_{\nu }.} ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {dI_{\\nu }}{ds}}=j_{\\nu }\\rho -\\kappa _{\\nu }\\rho I_{\\nu }.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/aa007b0cdcc24484b6ec924b9487688b70d9e841)
If the radiation field is in equilibrium with the material medium, then the radiation will be homogeneous (independent of position) so that _dI ν_ = 0 and:  κ ν B ν = j ν {\displaystyle \kappa _{\nu }B_{\nu }=j_{\nu }} ![{\\displaystyle \\kappa _{\\nu }B_{\\nu }=j_{\\nu }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cc2d8acc675f5e074dc348f8b6917b2fcb09768a) which is another statement of Kirchhoff's law, relating two material properties of the medium, and which yields the radiative transfer equation at a point around which the medium is in thermodynamic equilibrium:  d I ν d s = κ ν ρ ( B ν − I ν ) . {\displaystyle {\frac {dI_{\nu }}{ds}}=\kappa _{\nu }\rho (B_{\nu }-I_{\nu }).} ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {dI_{\\nu }}{ds}}=\\kappa _{\\nu }\\rho \(B_{\\nu }-I_{\\nu }\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/aaa6205f2cea000ca7f4f3164123a19bd8e74732)
### Einstein coefficients
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=15 "Edit section: Einstein coefficients")]
Main article: [Atomic spectral line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_spectral_line "Atomic spectral line")
The principle of [detailed balance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detailed_balance "Detailed balance") states that, at thermodynamic equilibrium, each elementary process is in equilibrium with its reverse process. 
In 1916, [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein") applied this principle on an atomic level to the case of an atom radiating and absorbing radiation due to transitions between two particular energy levels,[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Einstein_1916-35) giving a deeper insight into the equation of radiative transfer and Kirchhoff's law for this type of radiation. If level 1 is the lower energy level with energy _E_ 1, and level 2 is the upper energy level with energy _E_ 2, then the frequency _ν_ of the radiation radiated or absorbed will be determined by Bohr's frequency condition:[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Bohr_1913-36)[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Jammer_1989_113_115-37) E 2 − E 1 = h ν . {\displaystyle E_{2}-E_{1}=h\nu .} ![{\\displaystyle E_{2}-E_{1}=h\\nu .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/5615846159ad1f84fb6bec2276a1a41ba5f393ff)
If _n_ 1 and _n_ 2 are the number densities of the atom in states 1 and 2 respectively, then the rate of change of these densities in time will be due to three processes:  

Spontaneous emission
     ( d n 1 d t ) s p o n = A 21 n 2 {\displaystyle \left({\frac {dn_{1}}{dt}}\right)_{\mathrm {spon} }=A_{21}n_{2}} ![{\\displaystyle \\left\({\\frac {dn_{1}}{dt}}\\right\)_{\\mathrm {spon} }=A_{21}n_{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/412cd89e2c892dd9e4faafa2f6c35271bea94565) 

Stimulated emission
     ( d n 1 d t ) s t i m = B 21 n 2 u ν {\displaystyle \left({\frac {dn_{1}}{dt}}\right)_{\mathrm {stim} }=B_{21}n_{2}u_{\nu }} ![{\\displaystyle \\left\({\\frac {dn_{1}}{dt}}\\right\)_{\\mathrm {stim} }=B_{21}n_{2}u_{\\nu }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/02a2efcbd2f32be0eb7af199ceb478405c4c91f8) 

Photo-absorption
     ( d n 2 d t ) a b s = B 12 n 1 u ν {\displaystyle \left({\frac {dn_{2}}{dt}}\right)_{\mathrm {abs} }=B_{12}n_{1}u_{\nu }} ![{\\displaystyle \\left\({\\frac {dn_{2}}{dt}}\\right\)_{\\mathrm {abs} }=B_{12}n_{1}u_{\\nu }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6a8896fe709fa81c15ec9a122cc3979e107d69e8)
where _u ν_ is the spectral energy density of the radiation field. The three parameters _A_ 21, _B_ 21 and _B_ 12, known as the Einstein coefficients, are associated with the photon frequency _ν_ produced by the transition between two energy levels (states). As a result, each line in a spectrum has its own set of associated coefficients. When the atoms and the radiation field are in equilibrium, the radiance will be given by Planck's law and, by the principle of detailed balance, the sum of these rates must be zero:  0 = A 21 n 2 + B 21 n 2 4 π c B ν ( T ) − B 12 n 1 4 π c B ν ( T ) {\displaystyle 0=A_{21}n_{2}+B_{21}n_{2}{\frac {4\pi }{c}}B_{\nu }(T)-B_{12}n_{1}{\frac {4\pi }{c}}B_{\nu }(T)} ![{\\displaystyle 0=A_{21}n_{2}+B_{21}n_{2}{\\frac {4\\pi }{c}}B_{\\nu }\(T\)-B_{12}n_{1}{\\frac {4\\pi }{c}}B_{\\nu }\(T\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0263fa47626e948f527e9938eda2bb2490cbf32c)
Since the atoms are also in equilibrium, the populations of the two levels are related by the [Boltzmann factor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boltzmann_factor "Boltzmann factor"):  n 2 n 1 = g 2 g 1 e − h ν / k B T {\displaystyle {\frac {n_{2}}{n_{1}}}={\frac {g_{2}}{g_{1}}}e^{-h\nu /k_{\mathrm {B} }T}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {n_{2}}{n_{1}}}={\\frac {g_{2}}{g_{1}}}e^{-h\\nu /k_{\\mathrm {B} }T}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/beed3f482045c610d8f31b228a694df992f108eb) where _g_ 1 and _g_ 2 are the multiplicities of the respective energy levels. Combining the above two equations with the requirement that they be valid at any temperature yields two relationships between the Einstein coefficients:  A 21 B 21 = 8 π h ν 3 c 3 {\displaystyle {\frac {A_{21}}{B_{21}}}={\frac {8\pi h\nu ^{3}}{c^{3}}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {A_{21}}{B_{21}}}={\\frac {8\\pi h\\nu ^{3}}{c^{3}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4af940086b76916004e17ed236b1a0f62be04dd1) B 21 B 12 = g 1 g 2 {\displaystyle {\frac {B_{21}}{B_{12}}}={\frac {g_{1}}{g_{2}}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {B_{21}}{B_{12}}}={\\frac {g_{1}}{g_{2}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2b9eabb84eede52a6000e4aff8749fe2631da564) so that knowledge of one coefficient will yield the other two. 
For the case of isotropic absorption and emission, the emission coefficient (_j ν_) and absorption coefficient (_κ ν_) defined in the radiative transfer section above, can be expressed in terms of the Einstein coefficients. The relationships between the Einstein coefficients will yield the expression of Kirchhoff's law expressed in the [_Radiative transfer_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#Radiative_transfer) section above, namely that j ν = κ ν B ν . {\displaystyle j_{\nu }=\kappa _{\nu }B_{\nu }.} ![{\\displaystyle j_{\\nu }=\\kappa _{\\nu }B_{\\nu }.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/237a4f421b8659612ab66e6b30c94f386446c504)
These coefficients apply to both atoms and molecules. 
## Properties
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=16 "Edit section: Properties")]
### Peaks
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=17 "Edit section: Peaks")]
Main article: [Wien's displacement law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wien%27s_displacement_law "Wien's displacement law")
The distributions _B ν_, _B ω_, _B ν̃_ and _B k_ peak at a photon energy of[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-KK-Peak-38) E = [ 3 + W ( − 3 e − 3 ) ] k B T ≈ 2.821 k B T , {\displaystyle E=\left[3+W\left(-3e^{-3}\right)\right]k_{\mathrm {B} }T\approx 2.821\ k_{\mathrm {B} }T,} ![{\\displaystyle E=\\left\[3+W\\left\(-3e^{-3}\\right\)\\right\]k_{\\mathrm {B} }T\\approx 2.821\\ k_{\\mathrm {B} }T,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e6e6fb67823160b6b2bb5120fa77be3d250c874)where _W_ is the [Lambert W function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambert_W_function "Lambert W function") and _e_ is [Euler's number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euler%27s_number "Euler's number"). 
However, the distribution _B λ_ peaks at a different energy[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-KK-Peak-38) E = [ 5 + W ( − 5 e − 5 ) ] k B T ≈ 4.965 k B T , {\displaystyle E=\left[5+W\left(-5e^{-5}\right)\right]k_{\mathrm {B} }T\approx 4.965\ k_{\mathrm {B} }T,} ![{\\displaystyle E=\\left\[5+W\\left\(-5e^{-5}\\right\)\\right\]k_{\\mathrm {B} }T\\approx 4.965\\ k_{\\mathrm {B} }T,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/db116c3edb71e73e5bef9a6fa4f7e4ecbba0adfd)The reason for this is that, as mentioned above, one cannot go from (for example) _B ν_ to _B λ_ simply by substituting _ν_ by _λ_. In addition, one must also multiply by  | d ν / d λ | = c / λ 2 {\textstyle \left|{d\nu }/{d\lambda }\right|=c/{\lambda ^{2}}} ![{\\textstyle \\left|{d\\nu }/{d\\lambda }\\right|=c/{\\lambda ^{2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4b293d22aa6300935576fc12d0907509a69bc44b), which shifts the peak of the distribution to higher energies. These peaks are the _[mode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mode_\(statistics\) "Mode \(statistics\)")_ energy of a photon, when [binned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histogram "Histogram") using equal-size bins of frequency or wavelength, respectively. Dividing _hc_ (387.770 μm·K) by these energy expression gives the wavelength of the peak. 
The spectral radiance at these peaks is given by:       B ν , max ( T ) = 2 k B 3 T 3 x 3 h 2 c 2 1 e x − 1 ≈ 1.896 × 10 − 19 W m 2 ⋅ H z ⋅ s r × ( T K ) 3 {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}B_{\nu ,{\text{max}}}(T)&={\frac {2k_{\mathrm {B} }^{3}T^{3}x^{3}}{h^{2}c^{2}}}{\frac {1}{e^{x}-1}}\\\\[1ex]&\approx 1.896\times 10^{-19}\mathrm {\frac {W}{m^{2}\cdot Hz\cdot sr}} \times {\left({\frac {T}{\mathrm {K} }}\right)}^{3}\end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}B_{\\nu ,{\\text{max}}}\(T\)&={\\frac {2k_{\\mathrm {B} }^{3}T^{3}x^{3}}{h^{2}c^{2}}}{\\frac {1}{e^{x}-1}}\\\\\[1ex\]&\\approx 1.896\\times 10^{-19}\\mathrm {\\frac {W}{m^{2}\\cdot Hz\\cdot sr}} \\times {\\left\({\\frac {T}{\\mathrm {K} }}\\right\)}^{3}\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cdf0a23d460868669176469d2d800c8e8eae927c)
with  x = 3 + W ( − 3 e − 3 ) , {\displaystyle x=3+W(-3e^{-3}),} ![{\\displaystyle x=3+W\(-3e^{-3}\),}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/941c8582b88a915f952765e1faffdae9b288bd39) and B λ , max ( T ) = 2 k B 5 T 5 x 5 h 4 c 3 1 e x − 1 ≈ 4.096 × 10 − 6 W m 3 ⋅ s r × ( T K ) 5 {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}B_{\lambda ,{\text{max}}}(T)&={\frac {2k_{\mathrm {B} }^{5}T^{5}x^{5}}{h^{4}c^{3}}}{\frac {1}{e^{x}-1}}\\\\[1ex]&\approx 4.096\times 10^{-6}\mathrm {\frac {W}{m^{3}\cdot sr}} \times {\left({\frac {T}{\mathrm {K} }}\right)}^{5}\end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}B_{\\lambda ,{\\text{max}}}\(T\)&={\\frac {2k_{\\mathrm {B} }^{5}T^{5}x^{5}}{h^{4}c^{3}}}{\\frac {1}{e^{x}-1}}\\\\\[1ex\]&\\approx 4.096\\times 10^{-6}\\mathrm {\\frac {W}{m^{3}\\cdot sr}} \\times {\\left\({\\frac {T}{\\mathrm {K} }}\\right\)}^{5}\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/499b9cc2a017436a891653c41dd134f7f572d24a)with  x = 5 + W ( − 5 e − 5 ) . {\displaystyle x=5+W(-5e^{-5}).} ![{\\displaystyle x=5+W\(-5e^{-5}\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b0d0fb21b1613a553311936717578c409625b813)
Meanwhile, the _average_ energy of a photon from a blackbody is E = [ π 4 30 ζ ( 3 ) ] k B T ≈ 2.701 k B T , {\displaystyle E=\left[{\frac {\pi ^{4}}{30\ \zeta (3)}}\right]k_{\mathrm {B} }T\approx 2.701\ k_{\mathrm {B} }T,} ![{\\displaystyle E=\\left\[{\\frac {\\pi ^{4}}{30\\ \\zeta \(3\)}}\\right\]k_{\\mathrm {B} }T\\approx 2.701\\ k_{\\mathrm {B} }T,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/538cd5d72259f8da2c70a860fdc3231a0df5a1e5)where  ζ {\displaystyle \zeta } ![{\\displaystyle \\zeta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d5c3916703cae7938143d38865f78f27faadd4ae) is the [Riemann zeta function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riemann_zeta_function "Riemann zeta function"). 
### Approximations
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=18 "Edit section: Approximations")]
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/RWP-comparison.svg/330px-RWP-comparison.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:RWP-comparison.svg)Log-log plots of radiance vs. frequency for Planck's law (green), compared with the [Rayleigh–Jeans law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rayleigh%E2%80%93Jeans_law "Rayleigh–Jeans law") (red) and the [Wien approximation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wien_approximation "Wien approximation") (blue) for a black body at 8 mK [temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperature "Temperature")
In the limit of low frequencies (i.e. long wavelengths), Planck's law becomes the [Rayleigh–Jeans law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rayleigh%E2%80%93Jeans_law "Rayleigh–Jeans law")[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Jeans_1905a-39)[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Rayleigh_1905-40)[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Rybicki_1979_23-41) B ν ( T ) ≈ 2 ν 2 c 2 k B T , B λ ( T ) ≈ 2 c λ 4 k B T . {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}B_{\nu }(T)&\approx {\frac {2\nu ^{2}}{c^{2}}}k_{\mathrm {B} }T,&B_{\lambda }(T)&\approx {\frac {2c}{\lambda ^{4}}}k_{\mathrm {B} }T.\end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}B_{\\nu }\(T\)&\\approx {\\frac {2\\nu ^{2}}{c^{2}}}k_{\\mathrm {B} }T,&B_{\\lambda }\(T\)&\\approx {\\frac {2c}{\\lambda ^{4}}}k_{\\mathrm {B} }T.\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/df6cedb7e21d9888959540726600ed46704b86f8)
The radiance increases as the square of the frequency, illustrating the [ultraviolet catastrophe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraviolet_catastrophe "Ultraviolet catastrophe"). In the limit of high frequencies (i.e. small wavelengths) Planck's law tends to the [Wien approximation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wien_approximation "Wien approximation"):[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Rybicki_1979_23-41)[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-Wien_1896_667-42)[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-43) B ν ( T ) ≈ 2 h ν 3 c 2 e − h ν k B T , B λ ( T ) ≈ 2 h c 2 λ 5 e − h c λ k B T . {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}B_{\nu }(T)&\approx {\frac {2h\nu ^{3}}{c^{2}}}e^{-{\frac {h\nu }{k_{\mathrm {B} }T}}},&B_{\lambda }(T)&\approx {\frac {2hc^{2}}{\lambda ^{5}}}e^{-{\frac {hc}{\lambda k_{\mathrm {B} }T}}}.\end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}B_{\\nu }\(T\)&\\approx {\\frac {2h\\nu ^{3}}{c^{2}}}e^{-{\\frac {h\\nu }{k_{\\mathrm {B} }T}}},&B_{\\lambda }\(T\)&\\approx {\\frac {2hc^{2}}{\\lambda ^{5}}}e^{-{\\frac {hc}{\\lambda k_{\\mathrm {B} }T}}}.\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/51be6ccce8476a150e860908e1c317c1a33aa005)
### Percentiles
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Planck%27s_law&action=edit&section=19 "Edit section: Percentiles")]

[Wien's displacement law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wien%27s_displacement_law "Wien's displacement law") in its stronger form states that the shape of Planck's law is independent of temperature. It is therefore possible to list the [percentile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Percentile "Percentile") points of the total radiation as well as the peaks for wavelength and frequency, in a form which gives the wavelength _λ_ when divided by temperature _T_.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-44) The second column of the following table lists the corresponding values of _λT_ , that is, those values of _x_ for which the wavelength _λ_ is ⁠ _x_ /_T_ ⁠ [micrometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micrometer_\(unit\) "Micrometer \(unit\)") at the radiance percentile point given by the corresponding entry in the first column. 
That is, 0.01% of the radiation is at a wavelength below ⁠910/_T_ ⁠ μm, 20% below ⁠2676/_T_ ⁠ μm, etc. The wavelength and frequency peaks are in bold and occur at 25.0% and 64.6% respectively. The 41.8% point is the wavelength-frequency-neutral peak (i.e. the peak in power per unit change in logarithm of wavelength or frequency). These are the points at which the respective Planck-law functions ⁠1/_λ_ 5⁠, _ν_ 3 and ⁠ _ν_ 2/_λ_ 2⁠, respectively, divided by exp(⁠ _hν_ /_k_ B _T_ ⁠) − 1 attain their maxima. The much smaller gap in ratio of wavelengths between 0.1% and 0.01% (1110 is 22% more than 910) than between 99.9% and 99.99% (113374 is 120% more than 51613) reflects the exponential decay of energy at short wavelengths (left end) and polynomial decay at long. 
Which peak to use depends on the application. The conventional choice is the wavelength peak at 25.0% given by [Wien's displacement law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wien%27s_displacement_law "Wien's displacement law") in its weak form. For some purposes the median or 50% point dividing the total radiation into two-halves may be more suitable. The latter is closer to the frequency peak than to the wavelength peak because the radiance drops exponentially at short wavelengths and only polynomially at long. The neutral peak occurs at a shorter wavelength than the median for the same reason. 
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/Solar_spectrum_compared_to_black-body.gif/500px-Solar_spectrum_compared_to_black-body.gif)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Solar_spectrum_compared_to_black-body.gif)Solar spectrum compared to black-body at 5775 K Percentile | Sun _λ_ (μm)[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck%27s_law#cite_note-45) | Black body at 5778K | 288 K planet _λ_ (μm)
